ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e207	Today's attitude is a result of the treatment of Document 8:119 S by the representative Rasmus Hansson to reduce our material consumption. I want to praise the Green Party for taking up this proposal and giving us an opportunity to reflect on the environmental impact of our consumption. In the vote, there are different majority and minorities, and I guess the different parties will explain their views on the matter. Earlier this week Schibsted launched The Second Hand Effect, where the Swedish Environmental Institute has calculated the environmental impact of the sales of used goods from five of Schibsted’s websites. There was a study that gave interesting reflections and results, and that intervenes directly in some of the suggestions in this setting, including: I, to produce a statistics on the environmental impact of our consumption. The study carried out by Schibsted shows, inter alia: the sale of second-hand goods at Finn.no results in a reduced CO2 effect of 0,5 million tonnes of CO2. It corresponds to emissions from all traffic in Oslo for 10 months. It shows that it can be argued that the study also has some weaknesses, that used sales could save the climate for large emissions, and that re-use actually works. An ever-increasing purchase party cannot be an overall measure. In many areas, reuse, repair and greater accuracy will also be an important part of environmental policy. That is why the issue we are now dealing with is an important contribution to raising awareness of the fact that production and material consumption are also seizing resources and contributing to large environmental emissions. The Committee acknowledges this and agrees that increasing consumption contributes to the emission of pollutants, the eradication of plant and animal species and greenhouse gas emissions. The Committee also agrees that it is an objective to facilitate sustainable development and that it must be done through long-term resource management, using resources in line with nature’s resilience. Since 1990 private consumption in Norway has more than doubled in real terms. Many people say that there are many positive aspects to increasing consumption, but that also gives cause for reflection. It is not that all economic growth puts greater pressure on the environment, but helping to reduce consumption growth is a vital part of the debate on how to achieve more sustainable development. The proposal has allowed us to address the issue of the growth of the sharing economy, both in Norway and internationally. There are many positive aspects of the sharing economy that will provide opportunities for better use of our resources. In combination with new technologies, sales of second-hand goods, interconnections and private accommodation can be examples of how free capacity in society can be better exploited. It may entail lower costs for individuals, but may also lead to lower resource costs. The Committee then recognises that we should not be blind to the fact that the sharing economy can also have negative aspects in fiscal matters and in the rights of workers. There is no objective of establishing a sharing economy that develops as a black economy on the other side of society. I think it is important to stress that seas, if we are facing a major shift to reduce climate emissions, are also a positive development in many areas. Technology is constantly evolving and, together with clearer environmental requirements, results in lower emissions. The use of heavy metals and environmental contaminants has been greatly reduced, as has the presence of local air and water pollution problems. But we have to recognise that it can do much more, and this proposal is one of several areas where there is the potential for us as consumers to contribute to less environmental pressures and reduce our own consumption of resources. An important part of the proposal is about a stronger right to return claims and the fact that it is easier and cheaper to repair a product instead of buying a new one. A large part of the proposal is also about improving consumer rights and information because well-informed and conscious consumers have great power. Providing better rights and also better information to consumers is an important part of the work ahead. The setting also addresses the question of purchasing pressure, which can be seen a little in connection with body pressure, and it is a question that is important not least related to commercial advertising. I would like to be allowed to comment on the proposals No. 2–7, from the Christian People's Party, the Central Party, the Socialist Left Party and the Green Party.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e242	The Labour Party and I have a fundamental belief that new technology will be the most important thing in solving the climate crisis – new ways of doing things that ensure that the world can withstand both population growth and global prosperity, while emissions are falling. For it confronts us with some ethical and moral dilemmas. If all people on earth were to consume as much as we nordics, we would need three Earths. We don't, we just have one that we have to divide by as much as we can. It goes without saying that in a world where we want everyone to get to the same level of wealth as us, we have to use resources differently. Of course, it's a lot about technology, but it's also about other things. Since 1990 we have become twice as rich, but have we become twice as happy? I don't think so, and I'm not sure that the most important thing we'll need in the future is increased growth and higher material consumption. Traveling these debates is not about going back to the past, as some say. No, it's about the future – what values we think should govern in the future. We have a government that I believe prioritizes errors, which chooses tax cuts ahead of well-being. It is also an environmental issue, because, as Marit Arnstad said, all growth does not have the same impact on the environment. I think it'll be a bigger footprint on the environment to give tax cuts, increase consumption, for the richest in Norway, instead of investing in school, health or culture. There are many reasons to oppose the government’s tax cuts – the environmental issue is also one of them. The same thing we saw in the debate on Sunday open shops. The government didn't realize that people wanted to have a day where things went a little bit calmer, where the pace wasn't the same. Fortunately, the voice of the people won, and we keep Sunday – until now at least – as a little different day of the week. The new word of the year will have to be ‘share economy’. I don't think we're in a debate without anyone ending up with a sharing economy as one of the most important points. I think we have to spend time discussing what it actually is for something, because it's used in a lot of different forms. But that we can use our cell phones or other technology to share more, that's basically a very good news, not least for the climate. From a consumer point of view, sharing goods is a very good idea. But sharing economy isn't something you're either for or against, it's there in different forms. From the Labour Party we are positive to a lot of it, but we are also very clear that this must not lead to social dumping or impaired rights for workers. A number of proposals are being promoted today. There are very many of them who have good intentions, which we are very much in agreement with, but which we have today decided not to stand behind, because we believe that they are too unexplained, and that some of them are better suited to the discussion about the state budget. One of the proposals that we have chosen to support is the proposal for halving food waste. In a world that's going to need all the food we have, we can't throw food to the same degree as what we're doing today. The sea of food waste must be the very symbol of the unfair distribution in the world. The French government has set in motion a targeted effort to halve food waste by 2025 – with strict regulation for the shops and the food industry. We believe the Norwegian Government should do the same. But now we have realized that that proposal today cannot be voted on because it has been promoted before the session. That is why I would like to inform you that the Labour Party is going to vote in favour of proposal No. 1, from the Right, the Advance Party and the Left, that this is something to be assessed in the context of the Circular Economy Storing Message. From the Workers' Party's side, we want to say today that we have great ambitions about what that message should contain when it comes to throwing food. The most important debate on consumption and growth is in connection with the treatment of the state budget, where we make the annual priorities. And it was a very bad signal when one of the first things this government did was to remove the sustainability indicators in the state budget. It is about 30 years since Gro Harlem Brundtland launched the term «sustainable development». We're going to be able to have a growth and a development without destroying the possibilities of neither nature, the climate, those who come after us, nor those who live at the same time with us. It's a perspective that we have to include in all of our political work.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e265	The representative proposal we are dealing with today is an example of the fact that even the best intentions can lead the wrong way. I have great respect for the commitment of the representative Hansson and the Greens/Greens/EFA to work for a better environment and climate. However, this case illustrates with all clarity the difference between those of us who believe that the objective of environmental policy is to limit damage to the environment as a result of economic activity, and those who believe that limiting economic activity and growth is an objective in itself. The representative proposal also shows the difference between parties who believe that a free market, knowledge and technology will play an important role in solving the climate and environmental challenge, and parties who believe that state control, prohibition, lower prosperity and less freedom are necessary for – as the representative Hansson tends to say – to avoid boiling the globe. The right and government base their environmental and climate policies on conservative values such as the precautionary principle and the generation contract. The right will always be prepared to discuss new measures that reduce the environmental impact of the production and consumption of goods and services. On the other hand, it is not as the proposer assumes that economic activity and prosperity are always a problem in themselves. There is no linear link between consumption and environmental pressures, on the contrary, developments in recent decades have shown us that it is possible to decoupling economic growth and increased emissions and environmental damage. Last night, the IEA announced that for the second consecutive year, the CO2 emissions from the world energy sector remain unchanged, despite the growth of the global economy. Technological development and rational regulation can change the behaviour of citizens who contribute to reducing the environmental impact of our consumption of goods and services, without using coercion and prohibition that slows down the development of our wealth. The reason why I stress this is that we are in a time of increasing unemployment and major changes. More than ever, it is important that we be able to maintain employment and value creation that ensures the welfare state for the future as well. Let me spend some time on some of the concrete measures proposed in the proposal. The proposer wants annual statistics on the global footprint of Norwegian consumption. The right is concerned with a knowledge-based environmental and climate policy and therefore the government is also actively working to improve standards and estimates of such footprints both in the EU/EEA and in the OECD. However, it is not possible at present to produce data that is good enough for them to be used to design policies or instruments. At best, such calculations will act as guides or illustrations for raising awareness, but at worst they may be directly misleading and lead to countermeasures that are counterproductive. Norway has strong consumer protection with a long warranty period and strict environmental requirements for products. In fact, Norwegian rules and regulations are stricter than what is common in Europe in terms of rights and guarantees. The lifetime and quality of products are likely to be determined to a much greater extent by consumer requirements and expectations for manufacturers than by any political decision in the Stortinget. That being said, it's important that when the day comes that we want to get rid of a product, material recycling and recycling should be the way we do it. When the proposer wants to restrict and prohibit advertising for legal products, I believe it is wrong to do so. This is also a policy that reveals a complete absence of confidence in informed consumers making good choices and that politicians and bureaucrats do not always know best. In addition, it is a restriction of freedom of expression and an ideological thought that testifies to the desire to take Norway back to a time I think most of us are happy to have left – with state regulation and governance of people and business. There are a lot of suggestions in the matter I have not now commented on. Many of them bear the mark of being less realistic and difficult to implement and, therefore, a clean marking of the position of the Greens. In any case, I would like to end my speech by thanking the author of the proposal for an important debate, and at the same time I would like to address the proposal by the Right, the Progress Party and the Left on measures to reduce food waste as it stands in the committee's vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e297	There is little doubt that the government is basing its environmental and climate policies on the responsibility of the administrator and the precautionary principle. Of course, it is not a problem to share the intentions of the proposer to reduce environmental pressures in the production and consumption of goods and services, but we must not forget that the main challenges we face in reducing greenhouse gas emissions and achieving a lower-carbon society are to decouple economic growth and increase greenhouse gas emissions. It is important that our environmental policy aims to prevent and reduce the environmental impacts of economic activity — and not to limit the scope of economic activity or private consumption itself. It is also important to note that the government and the cooperation parties have committed themselves to following up the work of the Green Tax Commission, which makes a number of recommendations on how the tax system can be used to distort consumption in a more environmentally friendly way. While Norway is currently producing high quality public statistics in terms of consumption, environment and greenhouse gases, it would not be appropriate to estimate environmental footprints until international, recognised methods and data are available for these types of calculations, and the level of precision would not be sufficient to design policies and legislation. As regards the extension of the warranty period, Norway has strong consumer protection, with a long warranty period and strict environmental requirements for the products. The introduction of a stricter regime will necessarily also result in higher prices of the goods. Finally, it can be stated that the government plans to present a national bioeconomy strategy, in cooperation with businesses, organisations and instrument operators, and there is little doubt that the bioeconomy can play a major role in exploiting our resources and our consumption in a much more optimal way than today. Of course, the Progress Party supports the proposal we are proposing.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e312	I would also like to commend Rasmus Hansson and the Greens to promote this proposal for a representative. I would also like to congratulate the rapporteur, Mr Arnstad, on his excellent work. Material consumption in Norway and in our part of the world must be put under a critical look. This has to be done in addition to improving products and increasing the environmental characteristics of products. We have no right to base a growth in consumption that most others can only dream of, either from a justice perspective or from an environmental perspective. Let me draw out an old chief, Professor Trygve Haavelmo. When he received the Nobel Prize in Economics in 1989, he was asked what he would have talked about if he had got 15 minutes with the world leaders. He said he would explain the dynamics of an exponential function, the formula for exponential growth. Other wise minds have visualized this by telling the story of a thorn where it grows a water lily. At first it has one leaf, but it doubles every day: day two, two leaves — day three, four leaves — day four, eight leaves, etc. After 30 days, the thorn is growing again. But when was it only half-grown again? Yes, on the 29th day. The lesson of this, which can also be transferred to ever-increasing consumption growth, is that the growth in consumption has not been a steady development, but an explosive development in our part of the world. It's not sustainable in the long run. Consumption growth often causes, if not always, an increased burden on the environment, and that is precisely why we need to have an ongoing debate on the global footprint of material consumption. That's also why we have to make it easier to trade used goods. Fortunately, we see a flowering of used shops in this country. We've gained a growing awareness of use and throwing. Manufacturers should also be required to be able to repair goods, as far as possible, and this should be stimulated by facilitating the framework conditions for repair activities. A five-year guarantee period for several product groups will have a positive effect on manufacturers’ adjustment. It's worth making something that lasts. We at Stortinget can do a good job, but here I would like to remind you that consumer power can also move mountains. Confident consumers who demand products that hold are at least equally important. The sharing economy, which is about to become a foothold, has become a positive trend. We need to look at the tax implications of this, and provide a positive stimulus to this trend. Advertising also plays a significant role, and we buy a good part we don't need – at least I do. Therefore, it is good environmental policy to take a closer look at the legislation on advertising. Repeat, for example: – together with the Centre Party, SV and the Green Party – our previous proposal that telephone sales should require active consent, not just have a reservation option. It's absurd in a world where many people have to go to bed hungry, that in our part of the world we're throwing huge amounts of fully usable food. A goal of halving the food throw in 2030 is moderate and affordable. Once again, I am pleased with the reminder contained in this proposal, and that the treatment has resulted in several important issues being given a majority. The rapporteur has already addressed the proposals on which the Christian People's Party is involved.

We have a boom scheme in Norway, which works well with relatively small resources. It's also important to reinforce this. It is important to keep in mind that we need to reduce the environmental impact of our consumption, and that requires a long-term and systematic effort to achieve it.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e379	The foundation of the economy is the natural resources, and the planet's resources are limited. Clearly, then, it is a central political task to prevent resources from being overexploited by a few at the expense of others. I would therefore like to thank the rapporteur and others who have spoken before me for our initiative on this proposal. Today, mankind is seizing large parts of the earth's surface to produce raw materials for our material consumption. The areas we've already used are, in addition, the most fertile and species-rich, many areas are now degraded by monocultures, and vital resources like water are becoming scarce in many places. This is about much more than climate. According to a new UN report, the global take-off of fossil energy, biomass and minerals is expected to triple in the period up to 2050. Therefore, the UN General Assembly in December also agreed that sustainable production and consumption is one of the new global objectives to follow up the Millennium Goals. The proposals we are debating now are about following up the government's support for these UN objectives with concrete measures at home. We know that the Earth's resources are limited, that nature has tolerance limits, and that our consumption drains resources out of societies and economies that need them more. Then we have to deal with that. But consumption reduction is also a profitable, exciting and technology-promoting project. It is well illustrated by some of the proposals that are being promoted today, such as: dividing by products and services which may in practice mean that society is getting more out of less because many can enjoy and benefit from the same product without buying it for themselves – or providing consumers with a longer period of protection, imposing stricter standards of quality and preventing goods on the market that are made to break up. It is also good for consumers, smart for the environment, and challenges producers to contribute to a more resource-efficient economy. Providing good framework conditions for repair, reuse and recycling is essential for a circuit economy to become slightly more than a motto. To the representative of the Right: to consider a proposal for stricter regulation of advertising in our public common spaces as a distrust of the consumer, I would say – to use the representative’s own words – is less realistic. After all, advertising spends billions on billions around the world just because it works. And we humans let ourselves be influenced by both neutral information and, of course, commercial advertising. Obviously, it's consuming. It is good that the Stortinget for the first time is debating the reduction of material consumption in Norway, but it is sad that the Left, Right and Advance Party have decided to vote against all the proposals that have been put forward in the case. The Labour Party is also voting down most of the reasons, as we have heard today, that there is a need for more assessment, even though most of the formulations are just beginning with wanting to investigate or more evaluation. The Central Party and the Christian People's Party are on several good proposals, but it is only the SV who agree with the Green Party that the UK should adopt a target of reducing consumption to a fair and sustainable level by 2030. Many parties are opposed to making our material consumption a political issue. And yes, it has been common to think for a long time – and I would also say, perhaps not least – that consumption is something private that politicians should stay away from. Consumption is private, but in a globalised economy our private consumption is also political. Both public and private consumption has a direct impact on the ability of others to live a good life. It's politics. The resources on which the globe has to live – the basis of our economies – we share with people close and distant and with future generations. And we share them with other species. Obviously, it's the issue of Biggest since how our consumption can be reduced without reducing the quality of life. We note, in particular, that the Left here chooses to stand out from a number of good proposals that respond to several of the biggest global problems of our time at the same time: resource scarcity, food shortages, loss of biodiversity and climate change. We in the Greens are sure that the majority of the Biggest will take material over-exploitation seriously as the majority have also recognised the consequences of acid rain and climate change. Then again I would like to thank you for many good contributions, an exciting debate, and I take this up with me the proposal of the Greens/Greens/EFA.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e410	It's rare you get the opportunity to discuss a topic that's so wide and so deep, and really a fundamental ideological question, like what we're talking about today. It's quite rare as a conservative politician that you get a natural opportunity to quote Edmund Burke from the pulpit, so I'll do it. One of my favorite quotes from Edmund Burke is: Society is a contract not only between those who live, but between those who are alive, those who are dead, and those who are still unborn. It is an important basis for this government that we are based on the administrative responsibility and precautionary principle. We've taken over a planet, and we're going to leave it to our kids in at least as good a condition as we took it from those who walked in front of us. It is very much about strengthening environmental protection and strengthening climate action, but it is also about doing so in a way that takes care of the economic basis and the welfare basis that we have inherited. Therefore, we must not use instruments in the fight against the environment that undermine our long-term ability to build a sustainable society, and that is why this is an important debate. It cannot be disputed – everyone agrees – that the pressure on the planet’s resources is unsustainable. We need to convert into a more resource-efficient economy. We know that global climate and environmental challenges require a green shift in the world and in Norway. Norway shall be a low-emission society by 2050, and it will bring about significant changes, including in the equipment of material consumption. The main challenge is to address the undesirable impacts of consumption and reduce the environmental impact of critical biological resources and ecosystem services. I think that both the Committee's good work on the matter and the debate here today show a great deal of agreement on the objective, but there are also clear differences in the view of what is going to happen in order to achieve the objective. I and the government are convinced that we need to use the market in the service of the environment, we need to use technology and technology development, and we need to stimulate that technology development in the service of the environment. It is not the government's policy to criticise or reduce the level of consumption in itself, as the Greens call for. The low-carbon society is not about refraining from consuming goods and services, but about responding to needs in smarter and less polluting ways. The Committee proposes the annual publication of statistics on the global footprint of Norwegian consumption. Footprint is a powerful communication tool that can show how Norwegian consumption affects other parts of the world. It is difficult to calculate such indicators. Good national data are required for production, imports and environmental impacts. In addition, data from production in other countries that are again influenced by large trade flows within the international division of labour are required. The footprints are therefore currently best suited to awareness and illustration, simply because the data base is not good enough today. More international cooperation and development is needed to establish such data access and good practices. Such work is carried out in, for example: The OECD, the EU and the Nordic Council of Ministers. We aim to follow up the Storting decision in cooperation with the Statistical Central Bureau by calculating and publishing footprint indicators where possible and appropriate. The objective will be to develop indicators in line with methodological developments internationally. The Committee also proposes to strengthen the right to return under the Consumer Purchases Act by providing several product groups with a five-year limitation period. The Committee assumes that certain categories of goods have a five-year limitation period, but the Consumer Purchases Act – which is otherwise the responsibility of the Minister of Justice – does not provide specific categories of goods with a specific period of protection. The purpose of the review period is to protect the consumer’s right to complain, not to regulate the durability of the product. However, insofar as producers adapt to the warranty period, it is important that goods that are intended to last significantly longer than two years are maintained for five years. The government is working towards the EU to influence the proposal for new distance selling rules in order to ensure that this is the case. We share the goal of halving the food throw, and we are doing a good job – in cooperation with professional organisations – to reduce food waste. A Memorandum of Understanding was signed in May 2015 setting out the framework for the work on a more concrete agreement, where we want to set reduction targets, find effective measures, identify sources and develop indicators across the food chain. We do not currently have sufficient basis to set a target, but it is part of the broad work that we are doing well.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e603	This proposal for a representative addresses the issue of an overall review of important cultural memories and that a comprehensive plan for this should be presented to the Stortinget. The latest communication continued both the level of ambition and the targets adopted by the Storting in 2005. Protection work is organised in ten conservation programmes, which are the main means of achieving the 2020 objectives. The revised national budget for 2014 granted 10 million. kr extra for fire protection of dense wooden housing and stave churches. The preservation programme for technical and industrial cultural memories, for medieval ruins, for rock art and for selected archaeological and cultural heritage also looks as if the objectives can be achieved. Other programmes, on the other hand, have a slightly greater challenge in terms of backlog. Actions under these programmes have therefore been given special priority in recent years, although the situation may still be demanding. These are statements that are easy to refute. Overall, the cultural memory budget has increased from 614 million. SEK in 2014 to 640 million. kr in 2015. Similarly, it has increased to 708 million. kr in 2016. This represents an increase of 4,1 and 10,8 pst, respectively. Among other things, the Cultural Memory Fund increased by 10 million. kr in the 2016 agreement, just to limit the backlog in the case of private-owned peaced cultural memories. Here the government announces that in the 2017 draft budget they will come back to a full review of the achievement of the target so far and an assessment of the way forward. In this way, there should not be a need for a separate proposal asking the government to do what they have already said they should do. The majority of the population also agree with this view, and I look forward to the review that comes with next year's draft budget.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e622	According to the Cultural Memory Act, cultural memories are defined as ‘all traces of human activity in our physical environment, including locations to which historical events, faith or tradition relate’. We know that there has been human activity in Norway for many, many thousand years, so you can be sure that the potential for cultural memories is extensive. These may vary from tombs via residential sites to recent industrial buildings, vessels or different types of churches and other buildings. In addition to museums, municipalities, counties and countries, much cultural protection in Norway is organised and carried out by volunteers, by private individuals, or by non-governmental organisations. In this context, I will give all credit to all the volunteer souls who set aside time and resources for various activities to secure our cultural heritage for the future. However, the demand for maintenance of protected and protected cultural memories is high – has been high and is still high. Every year, some protective buildings or cultural landscapes disappear forever because they have not been restored. As far back as 2004–2005, Life with Cultural Memories, the review of the ‘Gross Communication’ concluded an overall increase in national cultural policy objectives. The annual loss of heritage and cultural heritage resulting from their removal, destruction or decay shall be minimised. By 2020, the loss shall not exceed 0,5 percentage points per year. Peaceful and protective cultural memories shall be ensured to have an ordinary level of maintenance by 2020. Cultural memories are publicly coordinated under the National Antiquities. For 2016 only, it has been granted more than 0.5 billion. kr for cultural memory and grants for protection and conservation in the cultural memory area. Since the accession of the Right-wing Party Government in October 2013, the appropriations for this work have increased by 100 million. kr, so although there is someone in this room who thinks that the government is not betting, it is actually that the appropriations have increased, by 100 million. kr. The Progress Party will continue to work to improve the coordination of cultural memory work by the public sector and to pursue the objectives set by the Stortinget for cultural memory work in Norway.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e810	The legislative proposals relating to the Energy Act presented by the Government are relatively extensive, affect the whole country and affect a number of companies and businesses. There are also principles in this case relating to future regulations and adjustments in the Energy Act on, among others. the external links between Norway and other countries with regard to the transmission of electricity. In this proposition, there are four specific areas in which there are amendments. It is a proposal to require a corporate and functional distinction between network activities and all other activities for all network carriers. Furthermore, it is proposed that the transmission system, which was previously the central network, become the highest level of the network and be defined by the Energy Act and that the Ministry may decide in individual decisions which facilities are part of it. The third amendment proposed is an authorisation to provide regulations on concessionaires under the Energy Act as coordinating distribution system operator. And the fourth is that there is a proposal to provide for the right to convert that deviates from the time limits of the Administrative Act in the event of errors in the data base in determining the income frameworks of the network companies. Let me start by saying that the last two points of the amendment that I referred to have not been a very big discussion about. The Committee does indeed consider it to be minor adjustments to existing legislation. Then there are the two other proposals mentioned, which have created greater commitment and greater discussion, and which have also been reflected in the Committee through greater uncertainty about both the basis and the consequences of the legislative proposals promoted by government teams. ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e869	The cord shift is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e872	Today, we are discussing proposals for amendments to the Energy Act in four areas relating to our common electricity network. The proposed changes are a follow-up to the Reiten Committee’s report ‘Better organised electricity network’ and an organisation for the implementation of the EU’s third electricity market package. Together, the proposals represent important changes that lay the basis for a forward-looking and climate-friendly energy system, and therefore I am pleased that the majority in this Chamber supports the legislative changes. Before I explain why we support the proposals, I allow myself a brief historical reflection. A little simplified, we can say that until 1990 the electricity supply in Norway was essentially just a matter of government. People got their power delivered by the local power plant, which was publicly owned. There was no competition between different companies to sell electricity to either households or businesses. At the same time, prosperity grew in Norwegian homes, and power consumption almost doubled from 1970 to 1990. Norway was well supplied with renewable energy after the large hydroelectric power build-up in the 1960s, 1970s and 1980s, but the need for power grid construction increased. In June 1990, Stortinget adopted the Energy Act, and electricity went from management to shopping. The law opened up a free market for the purchase and sale of electrical energy and thus also for an energy expansion more driven by the market and less by political decisions. The Energy Act already required power companies to distinguish between production and network activities. The deregulation of the power sector distinguished the physical supply from the purchase of electricity. According to Great Norwegian encyclopaedia, the Energy Act Norway placed the primary international focus on the liberalisation and release of electricity. With the new law, there were some structural changes and consolidations in the power industry, but it is still my claim that the Energy Act did not have its full effect until we arrived in the 2000s. Only then did the power market begin to function, and we are now on the threshold of a new change, a new age. The amendments to the Energy Act adopted here today are a natural extension of the deregulation in the 1990s and strengthen the interests of the consumer. Power is perhaps the most important infrastructure we have in today's modern society. We rely on power in the socket to almost everything we do in everyday life. Without a stable power supply, modern Norway stops up. In addition, we are in the middle of a green shift where renewable energy is an important factor in our success. Therefore, a well-functioning well-secured power supply network is critically important. There is little doubt that society as we know it today will change radically in the years to come. The energy sector is no exception. We are faced with a technological revolution the few know the scope of which. Evolution is faster than ever, and we see that this will have a dramatic effect on large parts of business in a number of ways. The power companies that are able to take advantage of the opportunities offered by these new technologies will have a significant advantage. We want to facilitate such a development. The basis for future power supplies is being created today. That is why we are now doing what we think is absolutely necessary changes to the Energy Act. In the context of the third electricity market package, we adapt the division of our electricity grid to harmonise with that of the EU countries. We're going from three to two web levels. What is now called central network is redefined into transmission networks. For the transmission network, ownership separation is required, which means that the transmission network will be operated and owned by the State network in the future. Which parts of the network to be defined as transmission networks are determined by the Ministry through individual decisions. Those decisions shall be based on network-based grounds and should be made by a neutral regulator. In this way, we ensure that the distinction between transmission and distribution networks is based on professionally sound reasons for the operation of the mains power grid. This is important, especially for those industrial enterprises that are large customers. The bill defines international connections, i.e. high-end subsea cables to our neighbouring countries, as transmission networks. The majority assume that foreign connections have a significant impact on the transmission network, but government parties also believe that other than State networks should be able to build and operate foreign connections in the future. The government has therefore sent a different amendment to the Energy Act at the hearing to allow such cables to be owned and operated by persons other than Statnet. In this context, I would like to stress that EU legislation allows for individual foreign connections to be owned by others than the transmission system operator, or the TSO, as it is called in the law. The majority, the Right, the Party of Advances and the Left therefore consider that the definition of the transmission system does not prevent individual foreign connections from being owned by other than State networks. As I said, the safe and good organisation of the mains is a prerequisite for a forward-looking and climate-friendly power system. The renewable society does not come about by itself – it actually needs to be built. In the near future, it's likely that many more of us will become so-called positive customers. This means that not only do we get electricity from our network company, but we can also sell surplus electricity from our own production back to the network company. In such a situation, we rely on the network company, which has a monopoly on operating networks in a particular area, to act neutrally and to ensure equal treatment for all those required to supply electricity in the network. Neutrality and transparency are becoming very important. We now come to the core of the disagreement between the majority and the minority in this matter. The operation of the mains network is a natural monopoly, and customers are not allowed to choose a network company. Since there is currently no requirement for a corporate and functional distinction between the operation of the mains power supply network and other activities in network companies with less than 100000 customers, there is the possibility of reducing costs to the monopoly activity. We believe that this cross-subsidy option indicates that customers need to be protected against unnecessary costs. We believe that close integration between network activity and activity related to the generation and placing on the market of electricity may impede an appropriate network structure and may result in higher network costs than is necessary. A corporate and functional distinction between networks and other activities helps to mitigate the disadvantages of integrated companies and will give increased attention to network activities. A sharp distinction, both in separate companies and with different people in government and management, I think will strengthen the network activity and raise awareness of the need for investment and upgrading the power grid. When we know that over the next ten years, the network industry is facing investments in new power grids and maintenance of over 100 billion. kr, it is important that it is invested properly. It is Right's view that today's organisation of the network is not forward-looking, with a combination of networks and other activities within the same energy company. If we are to ensure the best possible management of the mains network in the future, it is necessary that the network operate separately, and that energy companies wishing to operate with other activities keep this separate from the operation of the network. In the Committee's hearings and through meetings and written enquiries, we have received many input from the industry to this legislative change. The vast majority of those who are online customers, whether it's industry or business, are satisfied with the web as it is today, but they see that you can organize it in a slightly different way. The defenders of the status quo and the objectors of functional separation are the network companies and their owners who do not have such a distinction today. I can see that. Change is something most of us do not necessarily like, and concerns are about what this will mean for the company, and what costs it will entail, etc. To these, I would say that I understand the unrest, but for the Right, the concern for consumers' interests and for society's need for a well-organized electricity network is heavier. Many are also in favour of the proposed legislative changes. The NVE figures also show that the companies operating electricity networks alone have lower costs and are more profitable than integrated companies. One example of this is the merger of Fortum’s former distribution network activities in Eastfold, which has provided cost savings and improved delivery quality to customers. Finally, the amendments proposed here today constitute sensible steps to extend the work that started with the introduction of the new Energy Act in 1990. The changes are fully in line with the recommendations of the experts in the Reiten Committee, and they will equip our common grid for the future.

One of the proposals of the Reiten Committee was to introduce a corporate and functional distinction. We believe that this is a good proposal. Social separation implies that network activities and competitive activities must be distinguished into separate legal entities. Functional separation means that management cannot participate in both network activities and competitive activities and that the parent company cannot give instructions to the network activity on the day-to-day operation. ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e1314	In this case, the majority is in favour of requiring both a corporate and a functional distinction between network activities and other activities. This is supported by a number of hearing bodies. Those who support the proposal highlight, inter alia: that it will ensure neutrality between monopoly activities and competitive activities, including the prevention of cross-subsidisation and distortions of competition, and thus may result in lower and more appropriate network tariffs for customers. The NVE believes that it is essential to introduce requirements for both functional and corporate separation. The NVE claims that this will contribute to more efficient operation, development and exploitation of the network by facilitating structural changes that allow economies of scale to be exploited, by increasing attention to efficiency in network activities and by reducing the potential to burden network activities on costs associated with competitive activities. The riding committee also pointed out that companies making their decisions on the basis of maximum rational network operation and development will serve both power producers and power customers best. It is precisely the consumer perspective that has been one of the most important arguments that the majority now advocates a functional distinction between companies. Overall, larger network carriers have had lower network leases than the smaller ones. Today's Address Book provides a good overview of web rentals in Trøndelag. This shows that TronderEnergi Nett, as the largest and cheapest company, has a network lease of NOK 4000 lower for 20000 kWh than, for example, Kvikne-Rennebu Kraftlag. The electricity transmission market is a natural monopoly. Therefore, we believe that the national authorities have the task of facilitating the most rational operation, and that the consumer pays only for the goods purchased, namely the supply of electricity. We also know that companies that have a corporate and functional separation have lower operating and maintenance costs for each kilometre of built-up networks, even when it is controlled for size and location. In addition, we see that average dividends to owners are higher in companies with distinctions. If we are to succeed in this country's renewable effort, we are also completely dependent on the fair delivery of its electricity to new power producers. It is easy to imagine that a company that owns both networks and delivers power will favour its own power generation, and that it can become more difficult for, for example: plus customers to get power delivered online. The majority have chosen not to impose reception obligations on the plus customers, as we see that part of the discussion as a natural part of the energy message that comes later. Since February this year, the NVE has granted a general exemption that makes it easier to become a positive customer. The scheme depends on joint agreement between the network operator and the supplier. In the debate on the development of wind farms, there have been discussions on the costs of expanding networks and power generation. We choose to claim that these discussions will be easier if not the same company will both produce power and be responsible for network operation. Greater efforts on renewable energy production will require new investments or the upgrading of existing networks in several locations. We believe that fewer and larger companies will have better access to capital through, for example. loans in the bond market, and have better access to the skills to develop companies for the future. In addition, we believe that larger companies will improve security of supply and increase robustness in the supply of power, which is also important for industry, business and new, large power customers, such as SMEs. in the transport sector. With these changes in the Energy Act, we believe that we are creating a more robust and transparent power sector, with greater readiness to tackle a wetter and wilder climate. We are helping to achieve the objectives of a more renewable society and to make use of the technological solutions of the future for both the big society, people and business. These legislative changes are essential for the green shift and to equip Norway to achieve its objectives in the Paris Agreement.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e1333	Once again, the blue government is given the opportunity to tighten up the requirement for a corporate and functional separation of power companies. However, the EU minimum requirements on corporate and functional separation for all over 100,000 online customers are being met. Today, the seven largest companies in Norway have over 100,000 customers and have functional and corporate distinctions. 34 companies have chosen to make a company distinction, while all 144 network companies are required to separate accounts. This means that we are currently operating in line with EU requirements and have a system that captures the fact that there is no cross-subsidisation. A functional distinction involves having one's own administration with one's own executive director, board of directors, audit, etc. This costs, it weakens ownership and increases bureaucracy. It is also supported by the proposition, which states that in the long term the overall benefit of the measure is expected to exceed costs — in the long term. Therefore, everyone should consider voting in favour of this proposal tonight. I had also hoped that industrial parks with their own network could have been exempted. However, we believe that the work on technology development that the industry itself is doing needs to be complemented, and we believe that it should be launched a dedicated technology development programme that brings low emissions to the base. That's our solution into this. We do not want to go along with what the representatives include in the proposal. ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e1610	The representative Karin Andersen then addressed the proposals she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e1614	Norwegian petroleum activities contribute large amounts to the Community. Although the industry no longer has the same growth impulses, for many decades to come it will be one of our most important industries. Through the tax system and the day-to-day business, it has provided us with a significant pension fund that benefits both current and future generations. The petroleum industry is now in a difficult transition period with low oil prices. Companies are actively working on cost reductions and technology developments that can in the long term enhance profitability. The main task of the authorities here is to pursue a long-term policy that facilitates companies' choice of the most efficient and profitable solutions. Environmental and energy-efficient solutions are important considerations that have been given great importance in the construction of the petroleum industry. More instruments are currently being used to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. Charges, emission allowances and other instruments motivate petroleum companies to apply energy-efficient solutions and reduce emissions. From 1991 there has been a high CO2 tax on the cap, and the tax today is 435 kroner per tonne for the use of natural gas. In addition, the EU ETS imposes obligations on allowances. The combination gives a total price of about 500 kroner per tonne of CO2. This means that the petroleum activity has the highest price of CO2 in Norway. This high price gives companies strong incentives to choose emission reduction solutions. Energy efficiency improvement will be one of the measures that companies can consider to reduce emissions. Emissions pricing is estimated to have reduced Norwegian greenhouse gas emissions from the petroleum industry by a total of 5 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent. This means that the emissions in this industry are 25 pst. lower than they would have been without the instruments, and energy efficiency improvement has contributed to it. In addition to general instruments such as CO2 taxes and quotas, several instruments are directly linked to energy efficiency improvement. Enova supports e.g.: measures to reduce energy needs and greenhouse gas emissions both for industry and for petroleum. Norwegian petroleum activities are covered by the European quota system. This means that additional measures in Norway initially provide us with additional costs without affecting global emissions. Representatives propose to introduce more favourable depreciation rules for energy efficiency improvement. As I said, incentives for energy efficiency improvement are already strong. Measures leading to a more efficient use of energy are therefore already highly prioritised by companies because of the high price of CO2 emissions. Technology is being developed over time, and we should facilitate industry's choice of the most efficient technology in terms of emission reductions. On the other hand, if the depreciation rules are to be drawn up as proposed by the representatives, it will be difficult without favouring specific technologies rather than other solutions that can be more effective. Favourable depreciation rules would favour procedures that would allow large depreciable operating resources to be listed. In designing the tax system, two important principles are that industries should be treated in a non-discriminatory manner and that depreciation should correspond to the economic impairment of the operating assets. These basic principles promote productivity and economic growth and strengthen the overall competitiveness of the economy. Specific depreciation rules will be an inefficient tool to promote technology development and economic growth. It will also rock the stability and predictability which are an important characteristic of the Norwegian petroleum tax system. The proposed actions of the representatives could also address significant control challenges for tax authorities. The Tax Directorate does not have the competence to assess which investments are energy efficiency-enhancing. In an overall extraction plan, it would be difficult to identify the means of operation that contribute to energy efficiency improvement. The measure will therefore lead to more red tape and a more complicated depreciation system, while at the same time limiting the effects. The current instruments already provide strong incentives for energy efficiency improvement and emission reductions in the petroleum industry. The cost of CO2 emissions is high for petroleum activities and higher than in all other industries. I therefore do not see the need to strengthen incentives for energy efficiency improvement on the base and believe that this is not the basis for designing special rules in the depreciation system for energy efficiency improvement.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e1730	Today, we are dealing with the National Health and Hospital Plan and three Document 8 proposals related to the content and issues of the plan. Documents 8 address the quality of birth care, the framework funding of maternity wards and women's clinics, the development of the public health service and the new network model for quality in hospitals – both from the Centre Party – and a dream promise for emergency medicine, psychiatry and regional innovation, from the Left. For the first time since the state took over the hospitals in 2002, a government has presented the Stortinget with such a comprehensive and concrete plan for the development of the specialist healthcare service. The size has been invited with the plan to provide the overall policy framework for the development of the patient’s healthcare. The plan will be the government's operational tool to equip the specialist health service to meet the challenges of the future. The main objectives of the plan are to create the patient’s healthcare system, to give priority to the supply of mental health and drug treatment, to renew, simplify and improve healthcare services, to provide adequate health professionals with the right skills, to strengthen quality and patient safety, and to make clear professional demands for hospitals, to improve task sharing and cooperation between hospitals, and not least to strengthen emergency medical services outside hospitals. The Committee agrees that good governance and planning are a prerequisite for good health care. The Committee agrees with the government to equip the specialist health service to meet the needs of the future in a country as long as Norway is special. It is about finding a good balance between what needs to be done centrally and what needs to be done locally. When conflicting interests make it difficult to agree on what is the best solution, the patient’s consideration must be more weighty. One of the main actions of the plan is to propose hospital names that clarify content and make conceptual use more unambiguous and consistent. Another approach is that hospitals need to work in networks to succeed in task-sharing and overall patient progression. The plan confirms that Norway will have a decentralised hospital structure with a spine of emergency hospitals throughout the country, and no hospital will be closed as a result of this plan. These acts shall have the assent of the majority of the Committee. We have a good public health service in Norway. Not all countries have such a capable, competent and dedicated health care professional. The Committee is committed to gaining sufficient knowledge of the need for health professionals in the years to come, and the majority consider the development of a staffing model for hospitals to be a useful tool, precisely to prevent the shortage of health professionals. Norway is also the country with the lowest mortality after myocardial infarction and stroke, and is among the countries with the highest survival after breast cancer and cervical cancer. A unanimous committee points out that undue waiting is bad for the patient and may reduce the possibility of obtaining the best possible benefit from the treatment. This week we got numbers for those who are waiting for treatment. When this government took over, about 254,000 were in line for health care. New figures – that is the joyous thing – show that 42 000 fewer are in line. While fewer people are waiting for health care in the specialist healthcare service, more and more people are being referred to for evaluation and treatment. It's a pleasure. However, we need to equip the specialist health service for the future. Norwegian society will undergo major changes by 2030. Developments in population, age composition, people's expectations of standard and quality will have an impact on planning. We're getting older, and by 2030 there will be over 300,000 older than today. So we're moving to the cities, and the disease picture in the population is changing. More than half of the lives lost are due to cancer, heart and masculinity, musculoskeletal disorders and mental disorders. The consumption of healthcare in them over 70 years of age is twice as high as among 40-year-olds. In the consultation of the plan, Specter concludes with that description and writes among other things.\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t ‘The plan provides a good basis for a more comprehensive and predictable development of the health and hospital sector in the years to come. The plan provides a thorough and reality-oriented description of the challenge picture with major demographic changes, future labour shortages, tearing technological developments while increasing public health expectations.’ A unanimous committee indicates that user involvement at all levels is important to ensure that the patient’s voice is heard, and the Committee also refers to the expert group of users, patients and relatives, who have made representations to the plan on the importance of being heard, young as the elderly. The plan proposes advice for young people at health care establishments, to which young people with disabilities are very positive. Medical research and innovation bring forward new, improved and gentle treatment methods. Research and innovation provide more new pharmaceuticals and advanced medical devices, such as robotics. The Committee supports the plan which proposes that patients should be more involved in clinical research and that it is important to facilitate the participation of all hospitals in and integration of research into patient treatment. Specialization is speeding up, and especially in the field of surgery, there has been a raging development of e.g. open abdominal operations, which have been replaced by peritoneal surgery, robotics and methods of intervention in the use of imaging. We'll all be operated on with new techniques, which gives us more gentle cases and fewer complications. The Committee has noted that, in the light of societal developments, the government will have a new modular model of specialist training. Specialist training shall be more flexible and adapted to the needs of tomorrow. The Committee believes that it is important for specialists who have broad competence in the front of municipalities and specialist health services, and we want to strengthen in-house medical competence. The plan provides for the creation of a new medical speciality aimed at emergency reception. The majority of the Committee on the Right, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left believe that the objective of the new speciality must be to enhance the quality of services and to satisfy as much as possible international requirements for the competence of emergency reception and observation posts. Today we have 51 hospitals in Norway with emergency facilities. The plan addresses challenges that small hospitals may have, whether it concerns the recruitment of specialists or there is little patient basis. At 14 smaller hospitals, emergency medical and emergency surgery was surveyed in 2013, and hospitals had an average of three immediate emergency hospitalisations in surgical wards and six in medical wards. This is challenging. The plan therefore outlined two scenarios for developing emergency services in smaller hospitals. The Minister himself has said that the scenarios are not premise or lead the development of hospital services further, as the Committee also agrees. The majority of the Committee from the Labour Party, the Right, the Advance Party and the Left point to the fact that the plan states that the development of the surgical profession necessitates a change in the organisation of the emergency surgical response in smaller hospitals. A number of local adjustments have been made to emergency preparedness in different health establishments over the past 10 years. Among other things, I mention: The deacon home, Lovisenberg, the Helgelands Hospital Mosjøen, Health Førde and the West Viken. The discussion of the plan has largely focused on the smaller hospitals and their functions and emergency services. Never have hospitals like Narvik, Lofoten, Volda, Stord or Flekkefjord had so many visits and received so much attention, and never has the involvement around the hospitals been greater. It's positive. The Committee has received many consultation opinions in connection with the examination of the Storting. Among other things, the Lege Association has recorded well, and several of these have been preserved in the setting. Several consultation opinions also address an anxiety about retirement and the loss of functions. The health service action says: «many of these local hospitals have been threatened by closures and have had very uncertain operating conditions for a number of years. Such uncertain framework conditions – more than the size of hospitals – create recruitment difficulties. When the plan detects that in the future small hospitals will have problems recruiting qualified surgeons, the plan itself has laid the groundwork for such difficulties. On the other hand, if a hospital has a reasonably safe future and foreseeable operating conditions, it is also possible to recruit a stable and well-qualified staff’. For the government parties, it has just been important to have an agreement around the plan that creates calm and predictability in Hospital-Norway. This applies in particular to areas around hospitals that have felt threatened by closure. The parties – with the exception of the Centre Party, which has its own plan – were invited to talk and dialogue. As I see it, the Workers' Party was most concerned with evading the plan, criticising the Minister of Health and promoting its own views. It is all right since the views are largely similar to those of the government, except that they want to keep the regional health businesses. The Christian People's Party did not choose to join since they have a different view of the role of the Storting regarding what is to be determined by the enterprises versus the Stortinge on the issue of decision-making of hospital functions in some hospitals. With the agreement between the Left, the Advance Party and the Right, we want the plan to help eliminate uncertainty for the future of the smaller hospitals by recognising their value and clarifying their requirements and content. We are committed to strengthening cooperation and the division of tasks between small and large hospitals, including the allocation of individual areas from major hospitals, as well as hospitation and rotational arrangements. It is important that small hospitals are guaranteed the economy for such a development. These lots agree with the proposal to define the four different types of hospitals proposed in the plan: regional hospitals, large emergency hospitals, emergency hospitals and hospitals without emergency facilities. The four regional hospitals will have the largest provision of regional functions and national treatment services in a health region, but this does not preclude large emergency hospitals and university hospitals from having regional functions. The working party also supports the intention of the plan to classify hospitals into these categories. The parties to the agreement believe that emergency hospitals should have emergency functions in internal medicine, anaesthetics in the 24-hour period, planned surgery, preparedness for surgical assessment and stabilisation and management of acute events. We also believe that emergency hospitals should have trauma preparedness and, in general, emergency surgery services whose housing patterns, distance between hospitals, car, boat and air ambulance services and weather conditions make this necessary. Several of the hospitals will need full emergency surgery because of long distances. It is important to point out that changes in hospital services should not weaken the overall supply of food. Hospitals currently offering emergency surgery will continue to do so when the patient’s needs so require and quality and patient safety are taken into account. The agreement also lays down the competence requirements for the child ward and the geriatric competence requirements for large emergency hospitals, which other parties also support. The Sickness Plan proposes to strengthen emergency medical services outside hospitals, and the Committee considers it important to ensure the dimensioning and quality of prehospital services. A modified division of duties between hospitals, if any, must lead to a simultaneous review of the capacity of the ambulance service. The Committee would stress the crucial role of the ambulance service in emergency preparedness. The air ambulance is important for the preparedness, effective treatment and transport of acute illness and injury. The members of the Right Party, the Advance Party, the Christian People's Party, the Left Party and the Centre promote a concrete proposal that the government should come back to the Stortinget with its own case on the base structure of the air ambulance in Norway. The national health and hospital plan outlines the follow-up of the Storting's treatment, and it is such that all enterprises are required to draw up development plans and quality assurance is required. The majority of the Committee, with the exception of the Centre Party, also considers that significant changes in the division of tasks should be made between hospitals and clarified in local processes and development plans. The majority of the parties to the agreement are concerned that healthcare businesses should have close dialogue with municipalities and other relevant actors in the work on development plans. We also ask that the government keep Stortinget informed about significant changes that have been promised until the business meeting. The majority of the agreement also wants a new governance model for specialist health services where regional health businesses are not included. The management model shall be amended after the adoption and functioning of the national health and hospital plan. We look forward to the Womensland Committee's investigation into alternative management models for hospitals. Finally, I will address management and quality in management. The Committee considers that it is necessary to impose higher requirements on managers and new requirements on managers in the healthcare sector in the future. Greater attention needs to be paid to quality, patient safety and overall patient outcomes across professions, departments and institutions. Several recordings in the consultation round refer to the importance of good communication and proximity to the leader. The complexity of hospitals is great, and good interaction across units is important in order to obtain good patient flow. The Committee supports the principle of local management. In the national health and hospital plan, the Stortinget is leading the way for major and important decisions and the development of Hospital Norway. It ensures that the hospital is equipped for the future and provides stronger democratic management of hospitals.

ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e2298	The aim of health policy is to promote health and prevent disease and ensure the necessary health and care services for the whole population. Norwegian hospitals should provide good and equivalent specialist health services to all those who need it when they need it. Today's debate was to clarify the future of our local hospitals. National health and hospital plans should lead to policy clarifications on quality, recruitment and education, organisation and rental of health services. The Health and Sickness Plan should clarify the hospital structure, prioritised large investments, done well for task allocation and national, regional and local functions at our hospitals. In this way, we could secure Norwegian hospitals, the good skills environments and the security of the inhabitants. The government’s proposal for a national health and hospital plan does not respond to this key challenge. The setting doesn't fix this either. National health and hospital plans do not ensure the political direction of the hospital sector and have been a plan that provides even greater room for non-people elected by regional health care agencies to resolve and decide on significant changes in our public hospital provision. The Central Party believes that Norway must take the goal of creating the people's health service. The health service of the people is something far more than the treatment of patients and much more than the narrow and individual objectives of the government for its health policy, the creation of patients’ healthcare services. The human health system is a cornerstone of our solidarity of well-being. The health care of the people should be a good thing for the whole society and for people in all situations of life, which provide the basis for business and business. Today, we have marked social health differences in the population of Norway. Hence, the right to equivalent access to hospital services, regardless of where a cage is in the country or who is, is important. Decentralised hospital provision is necessary to provide such equivalent health services. We have to take on serious social responsibility for health services. The health system must be able to discharge its social responsibility through a democratic and leadership reform and a new network model for the development of quality in hospitals. Norwegian hospitals need new guidance, organisation and funding. The Centre Party’s alternative model for the organisation and financing of hospitals is based on a clear and clear division of work and functional distribution between hospitals, where one has the centralisation of specialised provision and decentralisation of other services. Local hospitals must be ensured for their key supply by clarifying this in their national health and hospital plan. Developments must take place in cooperation between local hospitals, central hospitals and university hospitals in the region. To date, this cooperation has been strongly influenced by competition between hospitals and not by genuine cooperation for the benefit of patients. The common professional environment, rotational arrangements, hospitation schemes, common training programmes and common back-up schemes are examples of measures that can strengthen professional cooperation. The same quality requirements must be set for large and small hospitals and quality transparency must be available, but the quality requirements must be met in different ways. In the network model of the Centre Party for the Development of Norwegian Hospitals, we base ourselves on the fact that the minimum requirement for basic competence should be daily preparedness before internal medicine, surgery, anaesthesia, x-rays and laboratory services, as well as the supply of food. We do not support proposals for minimum requirements for an emergency hospital such as the Right, the Advance Party, the Left and the Labour Party are some of them. That emergency hospitals do not need emergency surgery, but at the same time must be responsible for surgical evaluations and stabilisation and also responsible for the most acute events – it is a proposal that is totally unprofessional. The central party believes that Norwegian hospitals are currently being hired in a way that does not provide confidence in the population. The experience with today's business model is that decisions about significant changes in hospital provision lack democratic legitimacy and lead to much unrest. The centre party will liquidate today's health insurance law. We suggest that Norwegian hospitals be run in a management model with elected national, regional and local governance. For the purposes of this model, all hospitals shall have fully-fledged rentals with professional, budgetary and personal responsibilities, and financial liabilities, including for the operation and maintenance of the building stock. We propose to move away from today's market-oriented funding approach, and that hospitals should be financed mainly within the framework. There is no reason that when sick people are in hospital, they have to be financed otherwise than in the case of sick people in the municipalities. In the hospital debate, it is often claimed that we have too many hospitals in Norway, and that a collection for fewer and larger hospitals will provide better quality and more effective patient treatment and less expensive operations. It is not correct. It cannot be established that, in general, larger hospitals provide hospital services of better quality than smaller hospitals. Nor have we seen many small hospitals in Norway compared to other countries. Small hospitals are generally not less effective. On the contrary, research on hospital mergers shows that there is no such covariance. Analyses of hospital mergers into major hospitals in Norway and the rest of the world show that hospital mergers do not make any profit cork when it comes to quality or cost. That's rarely the result that's mirrored, and Oslo University Hospital is an example of that. The centre party records that the national health and hospital plan does not contain any mandatory wording on the level of investment or investment needs. The fact that the health plan also does not take into account that Norwegian hospitals today have an occupation rate far above what is common in other countries is also a fact.
