ParlaMint-NO_2016-03-17.ana.ud297e2547	Making a national health and hospital plan in Norway was one of the things I was most happy to do when I joined as Minister of Health and Care. The aim was to present a plan for developing our hospitals to master the challenges of the future, so that everyone in Norway will have safe hospitals and better health care wherever you live. The goal was to give the Storting the best possible insight into the status and challenges of the specialist health service and to invite what is our top elected assembly to give the overall leadership on how hospitals should develop. The goal was to present a plan that was just building the patient’s healthcare service across the country. The aim was to have a national health and hospital plan that ensures a continued decentralised healthcare with a backbone of hospitals throughout the country, where hospitals work together as a team, and where the larger hospitals will help not least to provide a safe basis for the smaller hospitals. All these objectives are widely accepted in today's setting. I also wanted an open planning process, where we were to share knowledge, dilemmas, challenges and scenarios in full transparency, and where everyone with an interest in health and hospitals could participate in the public vocabulary. I believe that we have also achieved that objective. The debate on the national health and hospital plan has been high, and the involvement has been high — the intensity too — in the last two years. We have engaged users, professionals, municipalities and organisations, through both meetings and debates around Norway. They've been in samples, they've been in different expert groups, and the word change has sometimes even been so engaging that more than I've probably experienced being added to opinions you don't have. But it is still very good today to be able to end the debate in Stortinget now, so that the plan is completed, and we can start implementing it. Now we need to equip the specialist health service for the future, and it is essential that we agree on the challenges. There will be much more people in Norway. The SSB says we're going to be 5.9 million inhabitants in 2030. We're getting older, and the older ones are going to be a lot more. Older people over 70 years of age use twice as much healthcare as 40-year-olds. The population of the big cities is increasing. Young people and immigrants settle in the first place in cities. The pressure on urban health services is due to several older people, more immigrants and more immigrants. The challenges of districts are primarily linked to an increase in the number of elderly and increased demand for hospital services as a result. Some diseases become less threatening. Others are growing in size. In particular, there is an offer for patients in the main disease groups who will be under pressure. However, this changes the health provision of people with chronic diseases, cancer, mental health challenges and drug dependence, so that services are better equipped to cope with the increase in these patient groups. Treatment opportunities are increasing, but they also increase costs and the need to make the right and good priorities. We have more differences in health services than we should have in a country where there is a common perception that health services should be equal to everyone. Unwanted variation may be a sign of both lower quality and wrong priority. Many still wait unnecessarily long for absolutely necessary treatment. Proper competence in the right place is likely to be the main challenge for the health service of the future. If we continue to organise and use the specialist health service as today, we will need 27 pst. several employees in 15 years or 30,000 more annual works only in hospitals and 70000 more in municipalities. This challenge cannot only be met by training and employing more and more people in the specialist health service. We need to work smarter and better organise ourselves, use more technology and use health professionals better in patient-oriented work. The Sykeshu plan shows that both increased resources and higher migration rates in healthcare are needed to master the challenges and provide a sustainable health service for the future. In this first national health and health plan, the government has chosen to focus on what we consider to be the most important thing in this plan period, which runs from 2016 to 2019. It is to create the patient’s healthcare and continue to give priority to the provision of mental health and drug treatment, to simplify, renew and improve services, to provide adequate health professionals with the right skills, to strengthen quality and patient safety and to make clear professional demands for hospitals, to establish principles for a decentralised hospital structure, to define the content of the different types of hospitals, to facilitate better division of tasks, not least to work more together in teams and to strengthen emergency medical services outside hospitals. I note that there is a broad consensus in the Storting on the main content of the plan and that there is a great deal of welcome about most of the proposals and measures outlined in the National Health and Hospital Plan, and I am pleased about that. One topic has been the subject of strong debate here today, and that is the issue of emergency surgery in the future hospital. One of the main objectives of the National Health and Hospital Plan was to adopt definitions that clarify the content of different types of hospitals. The government’s wish is to facilitate the differentiation of emergency hospital functions in the future hospital — this in order to ensure proximity to the services that are most needed, and not least to ensure the safe quality and patient safety of all hospitals, whether large or small. Through the agreement concluded between the Right, the Advance Party and the Left, a majority of the Health and Care Committee agrees with the government's proposal that we should have four types of hospitals in Norway in the future: regional hospitals, large emergency hospitals, emergency hospitals and hospitals without emergency facilities. The contents of the four types of hospitals are defined and elaborated in the position of the Health and Care Committee. I'm very pleased that there is a majority in the Committee on Development in which there will be different types of emergency surgery at the smallest hospitals in the future, depending on local conditions. In the proposal for a decision of the Health and Care Committee, the Swedish Government asks that ‘houses that currently offer emergency surgery will continue to do so when the patient’s needs are met and quality and patient safety is ensured.’ This is fully in line with what was both my intention and that of the government, and I want to see that health care establishments take this into account in their development processes. The Health and Care Committee asks the Government to ensure that the scenarios described in the National Health and Hospital Plans are not to lead the local and regional development processes to be implemented, but it has not been the government's intention that the conclusions be given in advance. I would like to clarify to the companies that the conclusion in these scenarios regarding the content of the individual hospital should not lead. The Committee points out that there is a need for local hospital management and suggests that local management become the general rule in Norwegian hospitals. I am much more concerned with better and clearer management in hospitals, among other things. to ensure good communication and proximity to the Chairperson. I want to communicate the Storting's decision on this in the business meeting with the regional health companies on 4 May this year. As the Committee also points out, it is important that uniform management requirements of the law are met and that health care businesses have some flexibility to design management models adapted to local needs within the framework of the Stortinge. National quality requirements for maternity care are set out in the Annual Report on maternity care, for 2008-2009, and in the Directorate-General for Health A safe food supply. These are fixed. This means that the maternity wards must have anaesthetics and anaesthetics available on a daily basis and emergency and Caesarean surgery personnel. The supervisor also recommends that all give birth to midwives as early as possible in the active phase of their birth. This requirement is now actively working to meet in all health businesses. But the number of births varies considerably from day to day throughout the year. Birth care therefore imposes additional requirements on good manning planning systems. This is something most hospitals now have a lot of attention. Birth care is financed as another somatic specialist healthcare service through both basic and effort-driven funding. This has been a principle independent of governments. The funding mechanism is one of several instruments in health policy, but it is important for me to stress that the medical evaluations are to lead the healthcare system that is provided to the individual patient. The Committee concludes on a development where hospitals will work together as a team to help the larger hospitals secure the basis for the smaller hospitals. This is also fully in line with what I experience is the intention of the representative proposal on a network model for quality in hospitals. The government is asked to ensure that more planned surgery is moved to the smaller emergency hospitals where it is possible to do so, in order to enhance the surgical competence of these hospitals. The government is also asking to return to Stortinget with its own case about the base structure of the air ambulance in Norway. All of this is also a prerequisite for the government's plan. I see it as a strength for the implementation of the national health and hospital plan that the committee is so clear on this. Once the Storting has dealt with the National Health and Hospital Plan, the development of hospitals shall take place within the framework laid down by the Storing. The mission will be given to regional health enterprises at the Enterprise Meeting on 4 May. The work shall be carried out in accordance with the process requirements of the new national guidance document for development plans of health establishments which will also be available at the same time. I have heard that someone in the debate is trying to create the impression that what the Storting decides today, and today's setting does not make a difference. That's wrong. It will all experience in the follow-up of the Enterprise Meeting on 4 May. Today, a proposal to give the counties a time-limited project responsibility for further development of health and care services is also being considered. Today, we have two levels of responsibility for providing health and care services to the population. These levels also have a separate responsibility for planning and organising services in the best possible way. The proposal, as I see it, will further disintegrate this responsibility and could lead to more ambiguity and more complicated cooperation. In this context, I would also mention that the aim of the municipal reform is to shift more power and authority closer to the inhabitants. Major municipalities will also have better conditions to plan and organise services in a good way. I look forward to starting the follow-up to the National Health and Hospital Plan. I am pleased that there is a broad consensus on the value of a highly decentralised healthcare service in Norway in the future. There is a broad stir to clarify the important role that smaller hospitals will play in future healthcare. The national health and hospital plan provides for the development of hospitals where hospitals will work more in teams, and larger hospitals will help to secure the basis for smaller hospitals. Our common goal is safe hospitals and better health care wherever you live. In this way, this national health and hospital plan makes an important contribution to creating the patient’s healthcare. The size of the population has now, through treatment, joined a new public health policy, a new policy for health services in the municipality through the primary health notification, a new pharmaceutical policy and a new policy for specialist healthcare. In this way, this has given a major boost to the development of healthcare in the years to come, and the title of all this work is: the patient’s healthcare.

With the National Health and Hospital Plan and the Annual Quality and Patient Safety Notices, the Government has focused strongly on transparency and political awareness — and attention on quality and patient safety in national health policies. We are working on finding good models for the certification of hospitals, improving services and providing the patient with a more predictable offer. Quality and patient safety objectives that can be used in quality work at all levels shall be further developed. National quality requirements for treatment provision shall be established where this is necessary to ensure equivalent quality. We need to create better quality and safer services through hospital certification. A national network for professional audits in hospitals is also to be established so that professionals can study and learn from each other. Reducing the waiting time for patient treatment has always been one of the Right Tabacles. It is therefore very gratifying that new figures from the Norwegian Patient Register show that waiting time has been reduced throughout the country, that a total of 42 000 fewer are now in health queues. That Health Mid-Norway, which I represent, has the country's second lowest waiting time, is also worth noting. Finally, I would like to refer to the setting, which states that there should be ‘a clear division of tasks between large and small hospitals’, while saying that ‘tasks that can be decentralised’ should be transferred ‘from large hospitals to smaller hospitals’. By decentralizing what we can and centralizing what we have to, we help to combine availability and quality in a good way. For me, this clarification is important given the hospitals in their own county, More and Romesdal, where the travel distance between, for example. The hospital in Ålesund and the St. Olav hospital in Trondheim is large and sometimes unfortunately very demanding, due to weather and traffic conditions. It's incredibly important that we manage to see the whole, so that a county without a university hospital also has good deals.

And these aren't rare cases. In recent years, on average, helicopter operations have been requested to the Health Fonna area at least 800 times a year — i.e. every day, at least — and that in a health establishment without its own base. This is perhaps the most prominent in cancer treatment. Health establishments are required to establish prostate centres where all radical treatments are to be carried out. In addition, diagnostic centres are established to optimise the quality of diagnostic and curative and palliative treatment. The prostate cancer association believes that the number of radical treatment centres in Norway should be reduced from the present number. They believe that the maximum should be five to seven, justified by the total number of surgical prostatectomys, which are approximately five to seven. 1500 a year. So here's a patient association asking us to specialise more and remove this kind of service from several hospitals. It's an interesting notion from those who are affected by one of our most common cancers. They don't want any more hospitals treating their disease, they want fewer and more specialised departments. When you get seriously ill, you don't always look for the nearest hospital, one will do the best. One will have routine, experience and expertise. That doesn't mean you're going to lay down several hospitals, that means you're going to specialise in several departments. Everybody can't get the best in everything, but everyone can get the best at something. I believe that good task allocation and more specialisation are important in order to create good hospital services throughout the country. This plan and today's decision add up to that. For the first time, the patient is the main person when we discuss the future hospital in Norway.
